We have, for example, f(s5(0), s(O)) -+n s(O) + s3(0) because s5(0) +
s(O)
s3(0) + s3(0) (heres n denotes the n fold application of s).

-+n

There is a fundamental difference between the system R of Example
7.2.34 and the quicksort system. In the quicksort system, it is always possible to first reduce the instantiated left-hand side of a condition to normal form and then to check whether the right-hand side of the condition
matches the normal form. This is, of course, not true for R: If we are interested in the reducts of f(s5(0), s(O)) and we first reduce s5(0) + s(O) to its
normal form s6 (0), then we find that Z + z does not match s6 (0). This phenomenon arises because the right-hand side z + z of the condition contains
a defined symbol (and consequently, even for normalized substitutions T,
the term ZT + ZT is not necessarily a normal form). In order to exclude
such a behavior, Avenhaus and Loria-Saenz [ALS94] introduced the class
of strongly deterministic 3-CTRSs.
Definition 7.2.35 Let R be a deterministic 3-CTRS.
1. A term t is called strongly irreducible w.r.t. R if t(Y is a normal form
for every normalized substitution (Y.
2. R is called strongly deterministic if, for every rule l -+ r ~ Sl -+
t l , ... ,Sk -+ tk in R, every term ti is strongly irreducible.
3. R is called syntactically deterministic if, for every rule l -+ r ~ Sl -+
t l , ... , Sk -+ tk in R, every term ti is a constructor term or a ground
Ru normal form.

Note that it is undecidable whether a term t is strongly irreducible w.r.t.
R; see Proposition 7.1.14. But there is an important decidable syntactic
criterion: If ti is a constructor term or a ground Ru normal form, then
it is strongly irreducible. Thus every syntactically deterministic CTRS is
strongly deterministic. Syntactically deterministic CTRSs are a natural
generalization of normal CTRSs and the quicksort system shows that these
systems arise quite naturally.

Quasi-Reductive Deterministic 3- CTRSs
In the next definition, the ordering >- st is well-founded because >- is well7. 2.6

founded; cf. Lemma 7.2.4.

7.2 Effective Termination

207

Definition 7.2.36 A deterministic 3-CTRS (F, R) is called quasireductive if there is an extension F' of the signature F (so F ~ F')
and a well-founded partial ordering ~ on T(F', V) that is closed under contexts and that satisfies the following conditions for every rule
l-+ r -¢= Sl -+ t1, ... ,Sk -+ tk E R, every substitution 0": V -+ T(F', V),
and every 0 :::; i < k:
1. if SjO" ~ tjO" for every 1 :::; j :::; i, then lO" ~st Si+lO",

2. if SjO" ~ tjO" for every 1 :::; j :::; k, then lO" ~ rO".
Quasi-reductive deterministic 3-CTRSs were introduced by Ganzinger
[Gangl, definition 4.2] without mentioning that the original signature can
be extended. This, however, is crucial because otherwise propositions 4.3
and 4.4 in [Gangl] would be incorrect. If signature extensions are permitted, then these propositions are valid. That is why we decided to define
quasi-reductivity as stated in Definition 7.2.36. The original definition of
quasi-reductivity [Gangl, definition 4.2] is additionally based on reduction
orderings. However, it is unnecessary to insist that ~ must be closed under substitutions because conditions (1) and (2) in Definition 7.2.36 must
hold for every substitution 0". Therefore, the following lemma from [Gangl,
lemma 4.5] holds independently of whether ~ is closed under substitutions
or not.
Lemma 7.2.37 If R is a deterministic 3-CTRS that zs quasi-reductive
w. r. t. the ordering ~, then -+n ~ ~.

Proof The proof is routine.

D

In view of Section 7.2.1, it is quite natural to introduce the new notions
of quasi-simplifyingness and quasi-decreasingness.

Definition 7.2.38 We call R quasi-simplifying if it is quasi-reductive
w.r.t. a simplification ordering ~ (note that in this case ~ = ~st).
As a consequence of the definition, every quasi-simplifying CTRS is
quasi-reductive. We shall see later that the converse is not true. In contrast to quasi-reductivity, quasi-simplifyingness is independent of signature
extensions in the following sense: If a deterministic 3-CTRS (F, R) is quasisimplifying w.r.t. a simplification ordering ~ on T(:F', V), where F ~ :F',
then (F, R) is quasi-simplifying w.r.t. to the restriction of ~ to T(F, V).
The simple proof of this fact is left to the reader.
Definition 7.2.39 A deterministic 3-CTRS (F, R) is said to be quasidecreasing if there is a well-founded partial ordering ~ on T(F, V) satisfying:

The following examples show that even for 1-CTRSs none of the vertical
implications in Lemma 7.2.40 can be reversed. In essence, this is because
the conditions are evaluated from left to right in the "quasi-case."

Example 7.2.41 Let R1 = {a ---+ a {= b ---+ c}. On the one hand, the
system is definitely not simplifying because no simplification ordering >-- can
satisfy a >-- a. On the other hand, the simplification ordering >-- defined by
a >-- b shows that R1 is quasi-simplifying (note that ---+n, = 0 and b -/tn, c).
In fact, this ordering also shows that R1 is reductive.
The CTRS R2 = {a ---+ d {= b ---+ c, a ---+ c} is neither reductive nor
decreasing because no partial ordering >-- can satisfy a >-- a. On the other
hand, the partial ordering >-- defined by a >-- b shows that R2 is quasireductive and hence quasi-decreasing (again, ---+n2= 0 and b -/tn2 c).
Examples 7.2.10 and 7.2.14 shed more light on the difference between
reductivity and quasi-reductivity. Consider the nonreductive 1-CTRS from

Corollary 7.2.59 IfR is a deterministic 3-CTRS, then the following implications hold:

U(R) is terminating

~ R

is quasi-reductive ~ R is quasi-decreasing

'* U(R) is innermost terminating.

Proof Combine Proposition 7.2.50 and Theorem 7.2.58.

o

According to Theorem 5.6.10(1), if U(R) is nonoverlapping, then all
properties in Corollary 7.2.59 are equivalent. It should be pointed out,
however, that even for strongly deterministic 3-CTRSs nonoverlappingness of U(R) is not implied by nonoverlappingness of R. Consider, for
example, the system R = {a --+ b ~ b --+ a} and its transformed system
U (R) = {a --+ U (b), U (a) --+ b}. The situation is different for syntactically
deterministic 3-CTRSs, which will be considered next.
Lemma 7.2.60 The transformed system U(R) of a syntactically deterministic 3-CTRS R is nonoverlapping ifR is nonoverlapping.

Proof Let h --+ rl and h --+ r2 be renamed versions of rewrite rules
from U(R) such that they do not have variables in common. If hand h
are left-hand sides of rules from R, then they cannot overlap because R
is nonoverlapping. If both hand l2 have a U symbol at their root, then
they cannot overlap either because of the shape of the U rules (U symbols
only occur at root positions and the root symbols of two different U rules

7.2 Effective Termination

225

cannot be the same). Thus let h be a left-hand side of a rule from Rand
12 = Uf(ti, Var(l), ... ). For an indirect proof, suppose that hand 12 do
overlap. Obviously, h must overlap with a subterm of k This, however, is
impossible because ti is either a constructor term or a ground Ru normal
form.
D
On the one hand, nonoverlappingness of R can be slightly weakened in
the preceding lemma. For example, we may allow infeasible critical pairs
as in the quicksort system by using the "optimized" U transformation explained in Example 7.2.49. On the other hand, the next example shows
that we cannot replace "nonoverlappingness" with "locally confluent overlay system."

Example 7.2.61 Let R = {a -+ b,a -+ c ~ b -+ c}. Because the second
rule ofR can never be applied, R is a (locally) confluent overlay system. But
its transformed unconditional system U(R) = {a -+ b, a -+ U(b), U(c) -+ c}
is not locally confluent.
Corollary 7.2.62 If R is a nonoverlapping syntactically deterministic 3CTRS, then all properties in Corollary 7.2.59 are equivalent. In particular,
a nonoverlapping syntactically deterministic 3-CTRS R is quasi-reductive
if and only if U(R) is terminating.

Proof In order to show that all properties in Corollary 7.2.59 are equivalent,
it is sufficient to prove that U(R) is innermost terminating ~ U(R) is
terminating. Because R is nonoverlapping, the same is true for U(R) by
Lemma 7.2.60. Hence U(R) is terminating according to Theorem 5.6.10(1).

7.2.9

Quasi-Simplifying Deterministic 3- CTRSs

In this section, we will provide several criteria that guarantee quasi-simplifyingness. The first uses the system Rq from Definition 7.2.46.

According to Lemma 7.2.6, simplifyingness of Rfib is equivalent to simple

Because both Propositions 7.2.64 and 7.2.68 are sufficient conditions for
proving quasi-simplifyingness, it is natural to ask whether one is subsumed
by the other. This is not the case, as the following examples show.

Example 7.2.69 Let R = {f(s(x)) -+ f(s(y)) -¢::: f(x) -+ f(s(y))}. It
is fairly simple to show that U(R) is simply terminating. To verify that
Proposition 7.2.68 is not applicable is equally simple; see [ALS94J.
Example 7.2.70 Let (F, R) be the 1-CTRS from Example 7.2.17 and let
(FU {c}, R) be its backward substituted system. R is obtained from R by
replacing the rule f(x) -+ x -¢::: x -+ d with f(x) -+ x -¢::: x -+ c. It can
be shown as in Example 7.2.17 that R is simplifying. Hence R is quasisimplifying according to Proposition 7.2.68. However, U(R) = Un(R) is
not simply terminating.
The following question is still open: Does quasi-simplifyingness of R imply that U(R) is terminating? If so, then one can show quasi-reductivity
of R by means of Proposition 7.2.50 whenever Proposition 7.2.68 proves
quasi-simplifyingness of R (hence Proposition 7.2.68 is somewhat superfluous if one is only interested in quasi-reductivity).

7.2.10

Summary: Methods for 3-CTRSs

Next we will briefly summarize the methods for showing effective termination of 3-CTRSs. In order to show effective termination of a 3-CTRS
R, one has to prove quasi-simplifyingness or quasi-reductivity or quasidecreasingness. R can be shown to be quasi-reductive by showing termination of the 3-CTRS Rq (Proposition 7.2.47). A more promising method
originates from [OhI99bJ. It consists of proving termination of the transformed unconditional TRS U(R) (Proposition 7.2.50). This method is advantageous because many sophisticated techniques for proving termination
of unconditional TRSs are known; see Chapter 5. Quasi-reductivity of R
implies innermost termination but not termination of U(R); see Theorem
7.2.58. In the special case where R is a syntactically deterministic nonoverlapping system, however, quasi-reductivity of R and termination of U(R)
are equivalent; see Corollary 7.2.62.

230

7. Conditional Rewrite Systems

Quasi-simplifyingness of R is equivalent to termination of the 3-CTRS
Rq U Emb(F) (Proposition 7.2.63). The following two methods, however,
are more relevant in practice because they can be used in combination with
well-known standard techniques like recursive path orderings: In order to
show quasi-simplifyingness, it is sufficient to prove simple termination of the
TRS U(R) (Proposition 7.2.64) or of the TRS (R)s (Proposition 7.2.68 in
conjunction with Lemma 7.2.6). The results On quasi-simplifyingness were
first reported in [Oh199a].

7.3

Confluence of Terminating Systems

In this section we will address the problem of proving confluence of effectively terminating CTRSs. As in the unconditional case, (conditional)
critical pairs playa crucial role in this context.
The critical pair lemma (Lemma 4.2.3) does not hold for normal 1CTRSs, as the following example taken from [Mid90, example 1.3.13] shows.

Theorem 7.3.2 Every quasi-decreasing strongly deterministic 3-CTRS
with joinable conditional critical pairs is confluent.

7.4

Orthogonal Systems

In this section we will study CTRSs, which are confluent independently of
(effective) termination. Throughout the section, every CTRS is assumed
to be orthogonal unless stated otherwise. To show that every orthogonal

7.4 Orthogonal Systems

233

CTRS is level-confluent, it suffices to show that these systems satisfy the
parallel moves property. This notion was coined by Yamada [Yam01l. As a
matter of fact, we will follow his presentation.

Definition 7.4.1 Let R be a CTRS. We write s*nnt ift can be obtained
from s by contracting a (possibly empty) set of pairwise disjoint redexes in
s by Rn. We write s*t if s*nnt for some n E N. The minimum such n is
called the depth of s *t. The relation
is called parallel rewrite relation.

*

Definition 7.4.2 Let R be a CTRS. We say that Rm and Rn satisfy the
parallel moves property if u = la ----tnrn ra = U1 and u *nn U2 imply the
existence of a term U3 such that U1 *nn U3 and U2 *n rn U3.
Proposition 7.4.3 For all C E N, the following statements are equivalent:

1. For all m, n with m + n ::; C, Rm and Rn satisfy the parallel moves
property.

2. For all m, n with m + n ::; C, *n rn subcommutes with *nn'

Proof The implication (2) =} (1) is obvious. In order to prove the converse
implication, we have to show for all m, n with m + n ::; C: If u *n rn U1
and u *nn U2, then there is a term U3 such that U1 *nn U3 and U2 *n", U3.
The proof proceeds by induction on the structure of u and case analysis.
If u = U1, then take U3 = U2. (Analogously, if u = U2, then take U3 = ud
If u = la ----tn", ra = U1 or u = l'a' ----tnn r'a' = U2, then the claim
follows from (1) because m + n ::; C. If the redexes contracted in both

write u = f(s1,"" Sj), U1 = f(h, ... , tj), and U2 = f(v1,"" Vj) for some
j :2:: 1, where Si reduces to ti (Vi) in one parallel Rmi step (Rni step) with

hypothesis that there are terms W1, ... , w j such that ti (Vi) reduces to Wi in
one parallel Rni step (Rmi step). Let U3 = f (W1' ... , Wj). Then U1 *nn U3
and U2 *n", U3·

Corollary 7.4.4 Let R be a CTRS. If, for all m, n E N, the systems Rm
and Rn satisfy the parallel moves property, then R is level-confluent.
Proof According to Proposition 7.4.3, *n", sub commutes with *nn for
all m, n E N. In particular, *n", is subcommutative for all mEN. This

conditional critical pairs, it follows that the CTRS from Example 7.4.9 is
level-confluent.

Level-Confluence for 3-CTRSs

Orthogonal almost normal 3-CTRSs do not satisfy the parallel moves lemma,
as the following simple example taken from [SMI95, example 4.4] shows.
Example 7.4.12 Let

Then f(a)-tftnla and f(a)-tftn2c but not a-tftn2c.
Note that R is a quasi-decreasing strongly deterministic 3-CTRS. Thus
it is confluent by Theorem 7.3.2. Next we will review a criterion that shows
level-confluence of 3-CTRSs like R. Of course, one has to make sure that
the extra variables on the right-hand sides of the rules are bound during
the evaluation of the conditions.
Definition 7.4.13 An arbitrary 3-CTRS R is called properly oriented if
every rule 1 -+ r -¢= 81 = tI, ... ,8k = tk in R satisfies: If Var(r) rz. Var(l),
then Var(8i) ~ Var(l) U u~::i Var(tj) for all 1 ::; i ::; k.
In contrast to deterministic 3-CTRSs, it is not required that Var(8i) ~
Var(l) U u~::i Var(tj) holds for alII::; i ::; k when the right-hand side r
of a rule 1 -+ r -¢= 81 -+ h, ... , 8k -+ tk does not contain extra variables.
Therefore, every deterministic 3-CTRS is properly oriented but not vice
versa.
By using an extended parallel rewrite relation, Suzuki et al. [SMI95]
showed the following theorem.
Theorem 7.4.14 Every orthogonal properly oriented right-stable 3-CTRS
is level-confluent.

Proof See [SMI95, theorem 4.6].

o

The theorem also holds for join systems and it can be extended in several ways. First, it can be generalized to almost orthogonal systems (note,
however, that at most rules of type 2 may overlay). Second, one may allow
infeasible critical pairs. Third, the theorem holds true for extended properly
oriented systems; see [SMI95, definition 7.1].
Note that Corollary 7.4.11 is also a corollary to (the extended version
of) Theorem 7.4.14.

8
Modularity

Modularity is a well-known programming paradigm in computer science.
Programmers should design their programs in a modular way, that is, as a
combination of small programs. These so-called modules are implemented
separately and are then integrated to form the program as a whole. Because TRSs have many important applications in computer science, it is
important (not only from a theoretical viewpoint but also from a practical
point of view) to know under which conditions a combined system inherits
desirable properties from its constituent systems. For this reason modular
aspects of term rewriting have been extensively studied in the past decade.
To render a detailed account of all known modularity results goes beyond
the scope of this book. Instead, we will give an overview of the most important results for TRSs (Section 8.2) and CTRSs (Section 8.7). We will
also present selected results in detail.

8.1

Different Kinds of Combinations

Definition 8.1.1 A property P is modular for TRSs if, for all TRSs
(:ri, Rd and (F2' R 2) having property P, their combination (union)
(F, R) = (F1 U F 2 , R1 U R 2 ) also has the property P.
The knowledge that (perhaps under certain conditions) a property P
is modular allows an incremental development of programs. On the other
hand, it provides a divide-and-conquer approach to establishing properties
of TRSs. If one wants to know whether a large TRS has a certain modular
E. Ohlebusch, Advanced Topics in Term Rewriting
© Springer Science+Business Media New York 2002

244

8. Modularity

property P, then this system can be decomposed into small subsystems and
one merely has to check whether each of these subsystems has property P.
First, we will show that all interesting properties are in general not modular.
Example 8.1.2 Let Rl = {a -+ b} and R2 = {J(b) -+ f(a)}. Both systems are terminating and hence normalizing. Their combined system R,
however, is not normalizing because f(a) has no normal form. The only
reduction sequence starting from f(a) is:
f(a) -+n f(b) -+n f(a) -+n f(b) -+n ...

Example 8.1.3 Let Rl = {a -+ b} and R2 = {J(a) -+ c}. Both systems
are confluent and hence locally confluent. Their combined system R, however, is not even locally confluent: f(a) -+n f(b) and f(a) -+n c but f(b)
and c are two distinct normal forms.
As all interesting properties are in general not modular, the startingpoint of research was disjoint unions, i.e., combinations of TRSs having
no function symbols in common. In his seminal paper [Toy87b], Toyama
proved that confluence is modular for disjoint systems. In contrast to
that, termination and convergence lack a modular behavior; see [Toy87a].
Kurihara and Ohuchi [K092] were the first to investigate constructorsharing systems-systems that have constructors in common. Middeldorp
and Toyama [MT93] introduced compos able systems, which have to contain all rewrite rules that define a defined symbol whenever that symbol is
shared. The authors, however, restricted their investigations to constructor systems. In [Oh195a], the constructor system requirement is dropped,
so the composable systems considered there are a proper generalization of
constructor-sharing systems. Hierarchical combinations of TRSs also generalize constructor-sharing systems and allow a certain sharing of defined
symbols; they were first studied by Krishna Rao [KR94, KR95a, KR95b]
and Dershowitz [Der95]. In practice, hierarchical combinations with a common subsystem [KR95a, KR95b] are most important because they generalize both composable systems and hierarchical combinations.
Definition 8.1.4 Let (F, R) be the union of (Fl' Rd and (F2' R2)' It is
easy to see that the set of defined symbols in (F, R) is V = VI UV 2 and the
set of constructors is C = F \ V, where Vi (Ci ) denotes the set of defined
symbols (constructors) in (Fi' R i ), i E {I, 2}.
1. (Fl' Rd and (F2' R 2) are disjoint if they do not share function symbols, that is, Fl nF2 = 0 (or equivalently C1 nC2 = C1 nV 2 = VI nC2 =
VI nV 2 = 0). The disjoint union (F, R) = (Fll±1F2' R 1 l±1R2) is sometimes also called the direct sum of (Fl' R 1 ) and (F2' R2)'

8.1 Different Kinds of Combinations

245

2. (Fl' Rd and (F2' R 2 ) are constructor-sharing if they share at most
constructors, i.e., Fl n F2 <;;;: C (or equivalently Cl n V 2 = VI n C2 =
VI nV 2 = 0).
3. (Fl' RJ) and (F2' R 2 ) are composable if Cl n V 2 = VI n C2 = 0 and
both systems contain all rewrite rules that define a defined symbol
whenever that symbol is shared; more precisely, the following inclusion holds: {I --+ r E R I root(l) E VI n V 2 } <;;;: Rl n R 2 .

4. (F, R) is a hierarchical combination of the base system (Fl' Rd and
the extension (F2' R 2) if Fl nV2 = 0 (or equivalently Cl nV 2 = VI n
V 2 = 0). That is, defined symbols of Rl may occur as constructors
in R2 but not vice versa.
5. (F, R) is a hierarchical combination of the base system (Fl' Rd and
the extension (F2' R 2 ) with common subsystem (Fo, R o), where Ro =
{I --+ r E R I 'rOot(l) E VI n V 2 }, and Fo = {f I f occurs in R o}, if
Ro = Rl n R2 and Fl n (V2 \ VI) = 0.
Definition 8.1.5 A property P is modular for disjoint TRSs if, for all
disjoint TRSs (Fl' Rd and (F2' R 2 ) that have the property P, their union
(Fl I±J F 2 , Rl I±J R 2 ) also has the property P.
In many papers a property P is called modular for disjoint TRSs if, for
all disjoint TRSs (Fl' R l ) and (F2' R 2), their union (Fl I±JF2 , R l I±JR2 ) has
the property P if and only if both (Fl' R l ) and (F2' R 2) have the property
P. We will focus only on the important if direction and will neglect the
less important only-if direction (which in most cases is very easy to prove
anyway). We will also use the phrases modular for constructor-sharing
TRSs, modular for composable TRSs, etc.
The first four kinds of combination are illustrated in Figure 8.1. The
relationships between them is depicted in Figure 8.2 (where the arrows
symbolize inclusion). It should be pointed out that compos able systems
are neither a generalization of hierarchical systems or vice versa. However,
hierarchical combinations with a common subsystem comprise both kinds
of combinations.

combinations of two systems.

D

For hierarchical combinations the situation is different because its definition is asymmetric. Here a base system defining some functions is given, and
one proceeds by defining new functions using the given ones in an auxiliary
manner. In an iteration of this process, the extended system will act as a
base system, which is further extended by new functions and so on. Note
that Examples 8.1.2 and 8.1.3 show that neither termination nor confluence are modular for hierarchical combinations. That is, further restrictions
are needed to obtain modularity results for hierarchical combinations; see
Section 8.6.

8.2

A Short History of Modularity Results

Because many modularity results have been published in the last decade,
it would be impossible to render a detailed account of all of them here.
Instead, the main results are summarized in Tables S.1-S.3, and we will
briefly comment on the tables. We would like to emphasize that the tables
are not exhaustive. The results are tagged with a reference to the article in
which they appear (note that some of them first appeared in preliminary
versions of the cited articles). Selected results will be treated in subsequent
sections of this chapter. Details on many more results can be found in the
dissertations of Middeldorp [Mid90], Ohlebusch [OhI94a], and Gramlich
[Gra96b], for example.

8.2.1

The Confluence Hierarchy

Let us start with the confluence hierarchy in Table S.l. The research on
modularity started with the seminal work of Toyama [ToyS7b], who showed
that confluence is modular for disjoint unions. A simpler proof of his theorem can be found in [KMTV94]. The situation changes, however, if one
considers TRSs with shared constructors. The following example originates
from Huet [HueSO] and was first used by Kurihara and Ohuchi [K092] as
a counterexample to the modularity of confluence for constructor-sharing
TRSs.

8.2 A Short History of Modularity Results

249

Example 8.2.1 Let

R

_ { F(x, C(X))
F(x,x)

1-

--+
--+

A
B

and R2 = {a --+ c(a)}. Both R1 and R2 are confluent and share the constructor c. We have

that is, the term F(a, a) has two different normal forms with respect to the
combined system R = R1 U R 2. Thus R is not confluent.
Sufficient conditions for the modularity of confluence in the presence of
shared constructors can be found in Section 8.5; cf. [Oh194bj. The material
includes a proof of Toyama's theorem.
Confluence is also modular for layer-preserving compos able TRSs; see
[Oh194aj. The union of compos able TRSs (F1' Rd and (F2' R 2) is layerpreserving if, for all rules l --+ r E Rd, d E {1,2}, we have root(r) E
Fd \ F 3 -d whenever root(l) E Fd \ F 3 -d; see [Oh195aj for more details.
Disjoint TRSs are layer-preserving if and only if they are noncollapsing,
whereas constructor-sharing TRSs are layer-preserving if and only if they
contain neither collapsing nor constructor-lifting rules (rules with a shared
constructor at the root of the right-hand side of a rule). The constructorsharing case will be treated in Section 8.5.
In a different context, Raoult and Vuillemin [RV80j showed that confluence is preserved under the combination of left-linear TRSs which are
orthogonal to each other (systems in which there is no overlap between a
rule from R1 and one from R2)' Consequently, confluence is modular for
left-linear constructor-sharing TRSs and certain hierarchical combinations;
see Section 8.6.3 for details. It is an open problem as to whether confluence
is also modular for left-linear compos able systems.
The modularity of local confluence for compos able TRSs is ascribed to
Middeldorp because it follows from the same principle as the respective
results in [Mid90j. As a matter of fact, it is easy to see that the union of
two locally confluent TRSs R1 and R2 is also locally confluent provided
that the equality CP(R1 UR 2 ) = CP(Rd UCP(R 2) holds. Note that composable TRSs and certain hierarchical combinations satisfy this equality;
see Section 8.6.3.
Modularity of strong confluence has not yet been examined. The following example shows that this property is not modular for disjoint systems. l

On the other hand, modularity of strong confluence for linear composable rewrite systems is a direct consequence of Theorem 4.3.2 because the
equality CP(RI U R 2 ) = CP(Rt) U CP(R 2 ) holds for compos able TRSs
Rl and R2 and certain hierarchical combinations; cf. Section 8.6.3.
Ground confluence is not modular because it is not preserved under signature extensions. For example, the system ({a,f}, {J(x) -+ x,f(x) -+ a})
is ground confluent. However, if we add a new constant b to the signature,
then the divergence a t- f(b) -+ b shows that the obtained system is no
longer ground confluent.
The normal form property is not modular for disjoint unions, as the next
example from [Mid89a, example 3.1] shows.

In contrast to that, it has been shown by Marchiori [Mar96a] that consistency w.r.t. reduction is modular for left-linear disjoint unions. This result
cannot be generalized for constructor-sharing systems, as Example 8.2.6
shows. A similar counterexample in which both systems have unique normal forms w.r.t. reduction can be found in [Mar96a].
Because hierarchical combinations subsume TRSs with shared constructors, every property that is not modular for constructor-sharing systems is
also not modular for hierarchical combinations. Example 8.1.3 shows that
the properties in the confluence hierarchy that are modular for constructorsharing systems are not modular for hierarchical combinations. Therefore,
further restrictions are needed to obtain modularity results for hierarchical
combinations; see Section 8.6.3.

8.2.2

The Termination Hierarchy

The modularity results of properties in the termination hierarchy are summarized in Table 8.2. In contrast to confluence, termination is not modular
for disjoint TRSs. Let us review Toyama's famous counterexample [Toy87a].
Example 8.2.8 The TRS 'Rl = {F(O,l,x) -+ F(x, x, x)} was shown terminating in Example 5.4.35. Its combination with the terminating TRS
R2 = {g(x, y) -+ x, g(x, y) -+ y} yields a nonterminating system because
there is the cyclic derivation

Naturally, the question arises as to what restrictions have to be imposed
on the constituent TRSs so that their disjoint union is again terminating.
The first results were obtained by investigating the distribution of collapsing and duplicating rules among the TRSs (in Example 8.2.8, Rl consists of
a duplicating rule, whereas R2 contains two collapsing rules). Rusinowitch
[Rus87a] first showed that termination is modular for nonduplicating and
for noncollapsing disjoint TRSs. (Drosten [Dro89] obtained parts of these
results independently.) Later, Middeldorp [Mid89b] proved that termination is preserved under the combination of disjoint systems provided that
one of the systems contains neither collapsing nor duplicating rules. One

It is easy to see that polynomial termination and w-termination are modular for disjoint unions provided the carrier sets of the interpretations coincide. This, however, cannot be generalized for constructor-sharing systems.
For example, the constructor-sharing TRSs F(a) -+ F(b) and g(b) -+ g(a)
are polynomially terminating but their union is not totally terminating;
see Example 5.3.8 and [Zan94]. These observations appeared in [MZ97] but
other authors may have made the same observations earlier. Note that our
definitions of polynomial termination and w-termination allow an arbitrary
subset N of N as a carrier set of the compatible algebra. w-termination is
nevertheless modular for disjoint TRSs because an w-terminating TRS always has a compatible algebra with carrier N as the following lemma from
[GMOZ02a] shows.

Lemma 8.2.10 Every w-terminating TRS (F, R)
monotone F-algebra with carrier 8et N.

0
It is unclear whether a similar lemma holds for polynomially terminating TRSs. Therefore, the question of whether polynomial termination is
modular for disjoint TRSs remains open.
Total termination is known to be modular for nonduplicating disjoint
systems. This result was first shown by Ferreira and Zantema [FZ96], and
a totally different proof was provided by Rubio [Rub95]. However, it is
unknown whether the nonduplication requirement can be dropped.
Modularity of simplifyingness for disjoint unions was first shown by Kurihara and Ohuchi [K090] and then extended to constructor-sharing and
composable systems in [K092, Oh195a]. The proofs of these results are
based on Lemma 5.2.29. As a consequence, for finite signatures or a finite number of rewrite rules, simple termination is also modular. Gramlich
[Gra94] showed that simple termination is also modular for finitely branching disjoint TRSs and conjectured that the finitely branching requirement
is superfluous. The proof in Section 8.4.1 shows that he was right; the proof
originally appeared in [Oh194c]. In Section 8.4.1 it will also be shown that
simple termination is not modular for constructor-sharing systems. Nevertheless, simple termination is modular for finitely branching constructorsharing systems [Gra94]' composable systems [Gra96b], and certain hier-

256

8. Modularity

archical combinations [KR94]. All these results will be reviewed in Section
8.6.l.
In fact, Gramlich's approach is based on the modularity of Ce-termination.
The notion of Ce-termination was originally called "termination preserving
under nondeterministic collapses" in [Gra94]. We prefer to use the shorter
phrase Ce-termination, which is formally defined as follows.
Definition 8.2.11 A TRS R is called Ce-terminating if the collapsing extended term rewriting system R l±J {Cons(x,y) --+ x,Cons(x,y) --+ y} is
terminating, where Cons is some binary function symbol that does not
occur in the signature of R.
Ce-termination plays a crucial role in proving termination in a modular way. It has almost the same modular behavior as simple termination
(see Sections 8.4.1 and 8.6.1), but it is by far less restrictive than simple
termination. As a matter of fact, in Section 8.3 it will be shown that DP
quasi-simple termination implies Ce-termination.
It was shown in [GA002] that (under a minor restriction) DP quasisimple termination is modular for disjoint TRSs but not for constructorsharing systems. The proofs of these facts can be found in Section 8.4.2.
There it will also be shown that DP simple termination is not modular for
disjoint systems. However, under certain natural conditions it is modular
even for compos able systems.
Nonself-embeddingness is not modular, as the following example from
[Gra94, example 5] shows.

In contrast to termination, innermost termination has a nice modular
behavior. This was proven for disjoint unions in [TKB95], for constructorsharing systems in [Gra95], for compos able systems in [Oh195a], and for
certain hierarchical combinations (with common subsystem) in [KR95a,
GA002]. We will review these results in Section 8.6.2.
Several authors (Bergstra et al. [BKM89], Drosten [Dro89], Kurihara and
Kaji [KK90]) have independently observed that normalization is a modular property of disjoint TRSs, and the same proofs show that innermost
normalization is also modular; see [Mid90] for details. We will show in

Toyama's counterexample also shows that neither nonloopingness nor

8.2.3

Combined Properties

Finally, let us consider the combination of properties from the confluence
hierarchy with properties from the termination hierarchy; see Table 8.3.
First, because local confluence is modular for composable TRSs, it follows
that convergence is modular for composable TRSs whenever termination is.
For example, convergence is modular for CE-terminating composable TRSs.
All those modularity results that can be obtained in the same fashion are
not listed in Table 8.3.
In Toyama's counterexample 8.2.8 to the modularity of termination for
disjoint unions, the system (F2' R 2) lacks confluence. Therefore, Toyama
[Toy87b] conjectured that convergence is modular for disjoint unions. Because confluence is modular for disjoint systems, this is equivalent to the
statement that the disjoint union of two convergent TRSs is terminating. But Barendregt and Klop disproved this conjecture. Their example
appeared in [Toy87a]. Independently, Drosten [Dro89] gave the following
simpler counterexample to the modularity of convergence.

Nice modularity results can be obtained by combining the modularity
of innermost termination and local confluence with the fact that termination and innermost termination are equivalent in locally confluent overlay
systems; cf. Theorem 5.6.8. An immediate consequence is that convergence
is modular for composable overlay systems because the combination of
two compos able convergent overlay systems yields an innermost terminating and locally confluent overlay system. Because constructor systems and
nonoverlapping systems are special overlay systems, convergence is modular for these systems as well. These results can be extended to certain
hierarchical combinations; see Section 8.6.3.

8.3 Cs- Termination and the Termination Hierarchy

259

It is surprising that convergence is not modular for variable-preserving
disjoint systems. The counterexample is a modification of Example 8.2.9.
It is too complex to be repeated here; interested readers are referred to
[Oh195c].
It has been shown in [SSMP95] that termination is modular for the following classes of left-linear TRSs: consistent systems, systems that are consistent w.r.t. reduction, and variable-preserving TRSs. None of these results
extend to the constructor-sharing case, as Example 8.2.14 shows (in order
to get a variable-preserving system, one may drop the rule F(x, y, z) -+ 2).
The main result in [SSMP95] also proves the fact that convergence is modular for left-linear disjoint TRSs: Because local confluence is modular and
termination is modular for left-linear TRSs that are consistent w.r.t. reduction, it follows that the combined system of two convergent TRSs is locally
confluent and terminating (hence convergent).
Because both confluence and normalization are modular for disjoint systems, their combination "unique normalization" is modular for those systems as well. The same argument does not apply, however, to TRSs with
shared constructors because confluence is not modular in this case. Nevertheless, unique normalization is modular for this kind of combination, as
we will show in Section 8.5.2. It is even modular for compos able TRSs and
certain hierarchical combinations; see [Oh195a, KR95b].
A glance at Tables 8.1-8.3 reveals that they do not contain a property
that is modular for constructor-sharing systems but not modular for composable systems. In fact, no natural property with such a modular behavior
is known.
It has already been mentioned that the abundance of modularity results
cannot be reviewed in every facet. Apart from those results that have already been mentioned, recent papers on modularity include [Pre94, F J95,
AT97a, Der97, Mar98].

8.4.1 C£ and Simple Termination
This section addresses the question of sufficient conditions for the modularity of termination of TRSs. The material presented here originates from
[OhI94c]. We begin by describing a proof technique that is often applicable
and that will subsequently be used in different variants. Let R1 and R2 be
disjoint terminating TRSs that have the same special property (e.g. they
are nonduplicating). If we want to show modularity of termination for TRSs
with this special property, we have to first guarantee that R = R1 ttl R2
inherits the special property from its constituent TRSs (otherwise we only
have a sufficient condition for the preservation of termination under disjoint union because we cannot infer the preservation of termination if more
than two TRSs are combined). In order to prove that the disjoint union
R = R1 ttl R2 is again terminating, we proceed by contradiction. Suppose
that there is an infinite R derivation

D' : s~ -+ s~ -+ s; -+ ...
Because we assume that every signature contains at least one constant,
we can replace every variable in D' with a constant and obtain an infinite
R derivation D : S1 -+ S2 -+ S3 -+ ... in which every Sj is a ground
term. (In fact, this assumption is not essential; see [OhI94c].) W.l.o.g. we
may further assume that D is an infinite R derivation of minimal rank,
where rank(D) = rank(s1). That is, any R derivation of smaller rank
is finite. Let rank(D) = k. By our assumptions, it follows that for all
indices j, rank(sj) = rank(D) and root(sj) E Fd for some d E {1,2}
(Le., the terms all have the same rank and their root symbols have the
same color). In particular, -+ is terminating on T<k = T<k(F1 ttl F 2) =
{t E T(F1 ttl F 2) Irank(t) < k}. The crucial point of the proof is to find a
transformation function <Pd : ~<5.k -+ T(Fd), where

~<5.k = {t E T(F1 tt1F2) lrank(t) = k and root(t) E Fd or rank(t) < k}
so that

is an Rd derivation. If we can further ensure that <Pd(D) is in fact infinite, Le., infinitely many reduction steps occur therein, then we derive the
contradiction we are aiming at: We assumed Rd was terminating.

266

8. Modularity

What kind of properties does the transformation function il>d need to
have? If all the Sj in D are top black, then every top white principal subterm
occurring in D carries "black information" that may become relevant for
the following rewrite steps. Pieces of that black information may come
up to the top and become part of the topmost homogeneous black layer.
This process may create new redexes in the top black layer which may be
reduced in subsequent rewrite steps (in Example 8.2.8 the rise of the black
constants 0 and 1 to the top layer was necessary to enable the infinite
derivation). So the black transformation function il>1 has to extract the
relevant black information from the top white principal subterms and store
it in same fashion such that it can be selected by black rules whenever this
is necessary. Thus the search for il>d is the creative part of the proof.
In the sequel, we use a variant of the proof technique described earlier.
Parts of il>d, namely, the storage and selection (retrieval) of information,
can be done by adding some new storing operator and associated selection
rules to the TRS. This storing operator may be any binary function symbol
not occurring in the signature under consideration. Denoting this symbol
by Cons, we are able to store black information as follows: Terms h, ... , tn
are listed in the term Cons(t1' Cons(t2, ... , Cons(t n , Nil) ... )). In order
to enhance readability we will write (h, ... ,tn ) for this list of terms, where
( ) stands for the empty list Nil (as a matter of fact, we can use any
constant from Fd or even a distinguished variable z instead of Nil; see
[Oh194c]). Now every term tj can be selected from this list if we add the
("selection") rewrite rules Cons(x,y) --+ x and Cons(x,y) --+ y.3 We will
show that if the disjoint union of two terminating TRSs is nonterminating,
then one of the systems does not remain terminating after the addition
of the rules Cons(x, y) --+ x and Cons(x, y) --+ Y and the other one must
contain collapsing rules. This is essentially done by giving a transformation
function
il>d: ~<O;k --+ T(Fd I±J {Cons, Nil})

that transforms a presupposed minimal infinite R1 I±J R2 derivation D :
Sl --+ S2 --+ S3 --+ ... where rank(D) = k and root(sj) E Fd, into an
infinite Rd I±J C£ derivation

where C£ denotes {Cons(x,y) --+ x,Cons(x,y) --+ y}. As we shall see, this
"abstract result" has interesting consequences. The "abstract result" was
first shown by Gramlich [Gra94] for finitely branching TRSs; we will elaborate on his approach in Section 8.6.1. Because we have a tool for storing
3This idea appeared first in Kurihara and Ohuchi [K090], but they used a varyadic
operator.

8.4 Disjoint Unions

267

and selecting information, the question is how to extract the relevant information. In [Gra94] this is done for finitely branching TRSs as follows:
In the preceding situation, because -+ is terminating on T<k(:F1 1±J:F2 ), one
can apply Konig's lemma and conclude that each term t E T<k(:F1 I±J :F2 )
has only finitely many reducts w.r.t. -+. Let the Sj be top black. Clearly, if
t is some top white principal subterm of some Sj, each top black reduct of
t reveals some of the black information hidden in t. All of these are listed
as suggested earlier. Moreover, because there may again be same relevant
black information hidden in the top white principal subterms of the black
reducts, the described process is applied recursively. Thus the black information hidden in t is pressed out of it by doing all possible reductions in
advance. Needed black information is then selected only by means of the
rules Cons(x, y) -+ x and Cons(x, y) -+ y. Obviously, this approach fails
if arbitrary (that is, not necessarily finitely branching TRSs) are involved.
If we consider, e.g., the TRSs R = {A -+ B j I j EN}, then the constant A
carries the information {Bj IJ E N} w.r.t. -+R, which cannot be stored in
a term Cons(B 1 , Cons(B2' ... )) because terms are finite objects. The idea
is then to retain and rearrange all the black information contained in the
term Sl (from which the presupposed infinite derivation starts) in such a
way that needed black information can be selected by means of rules from
both R1 and CEo In our small example this would mean that we keep the
constant A as long as we do not know to which B j it finally rewrites.
We start the presentation of our construction by giving some needed
lemmas and definitions.

Theorem 8.4.13 paves the way for our main result.

Theorem 8.4.14 Let R1 and R2 be two disjoint terminating TRSs such

that their disjoint union R1 l±J R2 is nonterminating. Then R1 is not C[terminating and R2 is collapsing or vice versa.

conclude that there is no index lEN such that the sub derivation of D
beginning at Sl consists only of inner rewrite steps. Thus there must be
infinitely many -tnd steps in D. W.l.o.g. we may assume that every term

Theorem 8.4.14 can be paraphrased as follows: If R1 and R2 are disjoint
terminating TRSs, then their disjoint union R1l±1R2 is terminating provided
one of the following conditions is satisfied:
1. Both R1 and R2 are Ce-terminating.
2. Both R1 and R2 are noncollapsing.
3. One of the systems is Ce-terminating and noncollapsing.
The combination of Theorem 8.4.14 with Propositions 8.3.1 and 8.3.2 has
interesting consequences.

Theorem 8.4.15 For disjoint TRSs, we have:
1. Ce -termination is modular.

2. Simple termination is modular.

3. Termination is a modular property of non collapsing TRSs.

4. Termination is a modular property of nonduplicating TRSs.
5. Termination is a modular property of TRSs that are inconsistent
w. r. t. reduction.
Proof Let R1 and R2 be disjoint TRSs. We have to show that R = R1l±1R2
has one of the properties (1 )-(5) if R1 and R2 have the respective property.
(1) We know from Theorem 8.4.14 that the disjoint union of two Ce terminating TRSs R1 and R2 is again terminating. But we have to prove
that it is also Ce-terminating. That this is the case can be seen as follows:
Let Cons be a binary operator that does not occur in F1 I±I F 2. Because R2
is Ce-terminating, the same holds, by Lemma 8.3.3, for R21±1 {Cons(x, y) --'t
x,Cons(x,y) --'t y}. Hence we may conclude by Theorem 8.4.14 that
R1 I±I (R2 I±I {Cons(x, y) --'t x, Cons(x, y) --'t y})
=

(R1 I±I R 2) I±I {Cons(x, y) --'t x, Cons(x, y) --'t y}

is terminating. But this amounts to Ce-termination of R1 I±I R 2.
(2) Because R i , i E {1,2}, is simply terminating, the same holds for
Ri U Emb(Fi). By Proposition 8.3.2, Ri U Emb(Fi) is Ce-terminating. The
application of Theorem 8.4.14 to R1 U Emb(Fd and R2 U Emb(F2) yields

274

8. Modularity

the termination of (Rl U Emb(Fd) l±J (R2 U Emb(F2)), or equivalently, the
simple termination of Rl l±J R 2 .
(3) The combination of two noncollapsing terminating TRSs yields a
noncollapsing TRS. Its termination follows from Theorem 8.4.14.
(4) Clearly, the union of two nonduplicating terminating TRSs is again
nonduplicating. That it is also terminating follows from Theorem 8.4.14 in
conjunction with Proposition 8.3.1.
(5) The combination of a TRS that is inconsistent w.r.t. reduction with
an arbitrary TRS yields a TRS that is inconsistent w.r.t. reduction. Hence
the assertion follows as in (4).

Corollary 8.4.16 The disjoint union of two finite DP quasi-simply terminating TRSs is Ct;-terminating.

Proof The proof is an immediate consequence of the fact that a DP quasisimply terminating TRS is Ct;-terminating (Lemma 8.3.4) in combination
with Theorem 8.4.14.
D
However, modularity of DP quasi-simple termination is not a direct consequence of Theorem 8.4.14. The problem is the following. If we combine
two disjoint DP quasi-simply terminating TRSs Rl and R 2 , then their
union R = Rl l±J R2 is terminating. Thus, by Corollary 5.4.17, for each cycle P in the dependency graph ofR there is an AFS A and a quasi-reduction
ordering;: satisfying the usual constraints. However, this is obviously not
enough to infer DP quasi-simple termination of R (for example, ;: must
be a QSO). It will be shown in the next section that DP quasi-simple
termination is indeed modular for disjoint systems.
If we combine disjoint TRSs, then it suffices to show that each constituent
TRS is simply terminating, DP simply terminating, DP quasi-simply terminating, nonduplicating and terminating, inconsistent w.r.t. reduction and
terminating, or Ct;-terminating to infer that their combination is again
terminating. Finally, the next corollary states sufficient conditions for the
preservation of termination under disjoint union. The first condition is due
to Middeldorp [Mid89b].
Corollary 8.4.17 Let Rl and R2 be disjoint terminating TRSs. Their
union Rl l±J R2 is terminating provided that:

• one of the systems is nonduplicating and noncollapsing;
• one of the systems is simply terminating and noncollapsing;
• one of the systems is DP simply terminating and noncollapsing;
• one system is DP quasi-simply terminating and non collapsing;
• one of the systems is Ct;-terminating and noncollapsing.

8.4 Disjoint Unions

275

Proof The proof is an immediate consequence of Theorem 8.4.14 in conjunction with Propositions 8.3.1 and 8.3.2 and the implications ST =} DPST =} DP-QST =} CET.
D

We have seen that, in contrast to termination, CE and simple termination are modular for disjoint TRSs. But does this result carryover to
constructor-sharing systems? Gramlich [Gra94] proved that CE and simple
termination are modular for finitely branching TRSs with shared constructors. The next counterexamples taken from [OhI94c] show that this extra
condition is necessary.
Example 8.4.18 Consider the TRS Rl = {Fj(cj, x) -+ FJ+l(X, x)
Fj(x,y) -+ x,Fj(x,y) -+ y I j E N+} and R2 = {a -+ Cj I j E N+}. The
systems share the constructors {Cj I j E N+}. Rl is terminating, hence
simply terminating by Lemma 5.2.30 (and thus also CE-terminating). The
same is true for R2 but their combined system R is not terminating:

F1(Cl,a) -+R, F2(a,a) -+R 2 F2(c2,a) -+R, F3(a,a) -+R 2 •••
In the preceding example the TRSs share infinitely many constructors.
This is not essential.
Example 8.4.19 The TRSs Rl = {Fj(cj(x),x) -+ FJ+l(X,x),Fj(x,y)-+
x, Fj(x, y) -+ y I j E N+} and R2 = {h(x) -+ cj(x) I j E N+} merely share
the constructor c. Here cj (x) denotes the j fold application of c. Rl and
R2 exhibit the same behavior as the TRSs in Example 8.4.18.

As already mentioned, Kurihara and Ohuchi [K092] proved that the simplifying property is modular for TRSs with shared constructors. For TRSs
over finite signatures, this implies that simple termination is modular for
constructor-sharing systems. It may come as a surprise that C£-termination
is not modular for this class of TRSs.
Example 8.4.20 Consider the term rewriting systems (Fl' Rd
({F,A,c},{F(cj(A),cj(A),x) -+ F(CJ+l(A),x,x) I j E N+}) and
(F2' R 2) = ({ h, c}, {h(x) -+ c j (x) I j E N+}). Both systems are CEterminating but Rl is not simply terminating. The combined system of
Rl and R2 is not terminating:

F(c1(A), c1(A), h(A))

Theorem 8.4.22 DP quasi-simple termination zs modular for disjoint

Example 8.4.23 also shows that restricted DP simple termination is not
modular for TRSs with shared constructors. It is easy to see that both

8.4 Disjoint Unions

Rl and R2 are restricted DP simply terminating. Their union, however,

terminating. In essence, this is due to the fact that one cannot use the AFS
rule b(x) -+ b' in the combined system Rl U R2 because of f(b(x)) -+ x E
R 1 . Therefore, to achieve a modularity result for constructor-sharing TRSs,
we have to restrict ourselves to AFSs that do not contain rules for shared
symbols like b. However, this restriction is not yet sufficient for obtaining
a modularity result for restricted DP simple termination of constructorsharing systems. We will not investigate this matter any further. The reader
can find sufficient conditions for the modularity of restricted DP simple
termination of constructor-sharing systems in [GA002].

8.4.3

Persistence vs. Modularity

In Section 5.5.3, persistence was defined as follows: A property P is persistent if, for every many-sorted TRS R, the property P holds for R if
and only if it holds for the TRS obtained from R by removing all type
information.
The next theorem, due to Zantema [Zan94]' relates the notions of persistence and modularity for disjoint (one-sorted) systems.

Definition 8.4.28 A property P is called component-closed if the following statements are equivalent for every TRS (F, R):
1. (F, R) has property P.

2. Every component of (F, R), that is, every equivalence class of the
equivalence relation
on T(F, V), has property P.

Bn

Most properties of TRSs (e.g., convergence, unique normalization, confluence, local confluence, termination, and normalization) are componentclosed.

Theorem 8.4.29 Every component-closed persistent property P is modular for disjoint TRSs.
Proof Let (Fl' Rd and (F2' R 2) be disjoint (one-sorted) TRSs, and let SI
and S2 be two distinct sorts. Define

i.e., the arguments offunction symbols from Fi must be of sort Si, i E {I, 2}.
The TRS R consisting of the rules from Rl and R2 with this typing is an
{S1, S2} sorted TRS. The terms of sort Si, i E {I, 2}, correspond one to one
to the terms over Fi and the reduction relation -+n on terms of sort Si
corresponds one to one to -+n i • Thus, the components of (F, R) can be

282

8. Modularity

viewed as the disjoint union of the components of (.1'1, Rd and (.1'2, R2)'
Because P is component-closed, it follows that

On the other hand, the terms over 8(.1') correspond one to one to the terms
over .1'1 I±J .1'2, and the reduction relation -+8(R) corresponds one to one to
-+R 1 ~R2' Therefore,

P(8(R)) {=} P(R11±J R2)'
Because P is persistent, P(R) and P(8(R)) are equivalent. All in all,

and we are done.

D

Therefore, persistence can be viewed as a generalization of modularity
for disjoint unions. It is an open problem whether the converse of Theorem
8.4.29 is true. On the other hand, it has been shown by van de Pol [PoI92]
that persistence is equivalent to the many-sorted extension of the notion
of modularity for disjoint systems.
Because termination is component-closed and persistent for noncollapsing or nonduplicating TRSs (Theorem 5.5.25), we immediately obtain an
alternative proof of statements (3) and (4) of Theorem 8.4.15 as a corollary
to Theorem 8.4.29. This alternative proof, however, turns history upside
down: First Rusinowitch [Rus87a] obtained the results and then Zantema
[Zan94] proved Theorem 8.4.29.

8.5

Constructor-Sharing Systems

In this section we weaken the disjointness requirement. That is, the TRSs
(.1'1, R 1) and (.1'2, R 2) are allowed to share constructors. In what follows,
(.1', R) denotes their union and -+ = -+R = -+R 1 UR 2 .
We will provide a sufficient criterion for the modularity of confluence
for constructor-sharing TRSs. This criterion can be extended to composable systems; see [OhI94a]. For the sake of simplicity, however, we stick
to constructor-sharing combinations. The most important consequence of
our criterion is certainly the modularity of confluence for disjoint TRSs.
As already mentioned, this result was first proved by Toyama [Toy87b],
and therefore it is sometimes referred to as Toyama's theorem. A simplified proof of Toyama's theorem appeared in Klop et al. [KMTV94]. In fact,
we will use the structure of this simplified proof. The material originally
appeared in [OhI94b].

8.5 Constructor-Sharing Systems

283

To be able to distinguish between symbols from different sets, we use
capitals F, G, ... for function symbols from VI; lowercase letters /, g, ... for
those from V 2 , and small capitals c, D, ... for shared constructors. As usual
x, y, Z, Xl, Yl, Zl, ... will denote variables. To emphasize that F = V I±I e,
we write ,(V,e, V) instead of ,(F, V) at the appropriate places.

and constructors and variables are transparent. If s does not contain white
(black) function symbols, we speak of a black (white) term. s is said to be
transparent if it contains only constructors and variables. Consequently, a
transparent term may be regarded as black or white; this is convenient for
later purposes. s is called top black (top white, top transparent) if root(s)
is black (white, transparent).
Several definitions and considerations are symmetrical in the colors black
and white. Therefore, we state the respective definition or consideration
only for the color black (the same applies mutatis mutandis for the color
white).

8.5.1

Confluence

In this section, R1 and R2 denote confluent constructor-sharing TRSs.
As noted in [Toy87b] and [KMTV94], the main difficulties in proving the
modularity of confluence are due to the fact that the black and white layer
structure of a term need not be preserved under reduction. That is, by a
destructive rewrite step a, for example, black layer may disappear, thus
allowing two originally distinct white layers to merge. Terms with a stable
layer structure will be called preserved.

Definition 8.5.8 A term S is preserved if there is no rewrite derivation
starting from S that contains a destructive rewrite step. We call S black
(white) preserved if all its black (white) principal subterms are preserved.
Clearly, a preserved term is both black and white preserved. If a top
black (top white) term is black (white) preserved, then it is preserved.
Note also that the properties preserved and black (white) preserved are
both conserved under reduction.

Definition 8.5.9 We write S -+c t if there exists a context C[ ] and terms
Sl, t1 such that S = C[Sl], t = C[t1], Sl is a special subterm of s, Sl -++ t1
and the root symbols of Sl and t1 have different colors. The relation -+c is
called collapsing reduction and Sl is a collapsing redex.
Note that every destructive rewrite step is a -+c step. On the other hand,
every -+c step contains at least one destructive step. Furthermore, -+c is
not closed under contexts in general because the notion "special subterm"
depends on the surrounding context.

286

8. Modularity

Lemma 8.5.10

1. If s -tc t, then s -t+ t.
2. A term is preserved if and only if it contains no collapsing redexes.

Proof The proof is straightforward.

In Example 8.2.1, we saw that confluence may not be modular in the
presence of constructor-lifting rules. The next example (a variant of Example 8.2.1) shows why collapsing rules have to be excluded as well.

Example 8.5.24 Consider Rl = {F(x, c(x)) ---+ A, F(x, x) ---+ B} and
R2 = {a ---+ g(c(a)),g(x) ---+ x}, which share the constructor c. Both are
confluent, but the term F(a, a) has two normal forms A and B.

Finally, Proposition 8.5.25 states that R is also normalizing.

8.6

Hierarchical Combinations

In this section, we gather modularity results about hierarchical combinations of TRSs. Because hierarchical combinations subsume constructorsharing systems, a property P can only be modular for hierarchical combinations if it is modular for constructor-sharing systems. In other words,
one cannot hope to obtain a modularity result for hierarchical combinations if P is not modular for constructor-sharing systems. In Sections 8.6.1
and 8.6.2, we will investigate the most interesting properties in the termination hierarchy that are modular for constructor-sharing systems, viz.
CE - termination, simple termination, and innermost termination. Part of the
material of Section 8.6.1 can also be found in [Oh102]. In Section 8.6.3, we
will consider local confluence, confluence for left-linear systems, and strong
confluence for linear systems. Convergence will also be treated there.

8.6.1

C[ and Simple Termination

The hierarchical combination of Rl = {a --+ b} and R2 = {f(b) --+ f(a)}
from Example 8.1.2 shows that simple termination is not modular for hierarchical combinations. On the other hand, the hierarchical combination of
the base system R+ and the extension R* as introduced in Example 8.1.6

8.6 Hierarchical Combinations

295

is again simply terminating:
R+

R*

={
={

s(x) + y

--+
--+

O*y
s(x)*y

--+ 0
--+ (x*y)+y

y
s(x + y)

What is the difference between these examples? That is, why is the hierarchical combination of R+ and R* so benign and that of RI and R2 so
malignant? In essence, this is due to the fact that on the right-hand side
(x * y) + y of the second rule from R* the function symbol + from V +
occurs above the function symbol * from V*' whereas on the right-hand
side f (a) the function symbol a from VI occurs below the function symbol
f from V 2 . This fact has been observed independently and contemporaneously by Dershowitz [Der95] and Krishna Rao [KR95a] and lead to the
notion of proper extensions; cf. Krishna Rao [KR94, KR95a, KR95b]. In
order to review the definition of proper extensions, we have to introduce
the dependency relation IO':d.

Definition 8.6.1 For a TRS (F, R), the dependency relation IO':d is the
smallest quasi-ordering satisfying the condition f IO':d 9 whenever there is
a rewrite rule f( ... ) --+ C[g( ... )] E R with 9 E V. So f IO':d 9 holds if the
function f depends on the definition of the function g.
Definition 8.6.2 Let (F, R) be the hierarchical combination of base system (FI' R I ) and extension (F2' R2). The defined symbols V 2 of R2 are
split into two sets V~ and V§, where V~ contains all defined symbols of V 2
that depend on a defined symbol of R I , i.e.,

and V§ = V 2 \ V~.
R2 is a proper extension of RI if every rewrite rule l --+ r E R2 satisfies
the following condition: If t is a subterm of r such that root(t) E V~ and
root( t) IO':d root( l), then t does not contain a function symbol from VI U V~
strictly below its root.
R2 is a restricted proper extension of RI if it is a proper extension of RI
such that no left-hand side of the rewrite rules of R2 contains a function
symbol from VI U V~ strictly below its root.
Roughly speaking, in a proper extension functions depending on RI are
never called within a recursive call of R2 functions. It is easy to see that
{J (b) --+ f (a)} is not a proper extension of {a --+ b}, while R* is a restricted
proper extension of R+. A more complex example is the following.

By using an appropriate AFS and rpo, it is not difficult to show that R2 is

to V~. Because avg does not occur on a right-hand side of a rewrite rule,
R2 is a proper extension of R I . Furthermore, because none of the symbols
+, ++, sum, and avg appears in a proper subterm of a left-hand side of the
rewrite rules of R2, R2 is even a restricted proper extension of R I . Note
that neither RI nor R2 is simply terminating.
As hierarchical combinations with a common subsystem generalize both
compos able systems and hierarchical combinations, proper extension with

8.6 Hierarchical Combinations

a common subsystem extend both composable systems and proper extensions.

Definition 8.6.4 Let (F, R) be a hierarchical combination of the base
system (F1 , Rd and the extension (F2 , R 2 ) with a common sUbsystem
(Fa, Ro). Define Va = {root(l) I l -+ r ERa}, Vi = V 1 \ Va and V~ =
V 2 \ Va. Now V~ is split in two sets V/~ and V/~, where V/~ = {III E
V~, I i2:d 9 for some 9 E Vi} and V/~ = V~ \ V/~. The TRS R2 is a called
a proper extension of R1 with a common subsystem Ro if every rewrite
rule of l -+ r E R2 satisfies the following condition: If t is a subterm of r
such that root(t) E V/~ and root(t) i2:d root(l), then t contains no function
symbol from Vi U V/~ strictly below its root.
R2 is a restricted proper extension of R1 with a common subsystem Ro if
it is a proper extension of R1 with a common subsystem Ro such that no
left-hand side of the rewrite rules of R2 contains a function symbol from
Vi U V/~ strictly below its root.
It should be pointed out that proper extensions with a common subsystem were introduced as generalized proper extension in [KR95a]. As a
matter of fact, Krishna Rao [KR95a] defined many more classes of extensions and it is not easy to keep track of all these different extensions. In
order to keep the presentation of the material clear, we will restrict ourselves to proper extensions (with a common subsystem).

Example 8.6.5 Let R1 be as in Example 8.6.3 and let R2 consist of the
rewrite rules of the second TRS in Example 8.6.3 and the rewrite rules of
R+. Then Va = {+}, Vi = {-H-,sum}, V~ = {-,quot,length,hd,avg},
V/~ = {avg}, and V/~ = {-,quot,length,hd},. It is readily verified that
R2 is restricted proper extension of R1 with a common subsystem R+.
In the following, we will derive modularity results that allow us to conclude termination of hierarchical combinations like proper extensions. Moreover, we will develop techniques to prove termination of TRSs incrementally. These are based on the dependency pair method. We need the following refinement of Definition 5.4.8.

Definition 8.6.6 Let (F1, R 1) and (F2, R 2) be TRSs. If there is an infinite sequence (81, t1)(82, t2)'" of dependency pairs from R1 and a substitution a : V -+ T(F1 U F2, V) such that tja -+RNR. 2 8j+1a holds for all
consecutive pairs (Sj, tj) and (8j+1, tj+1) in the sequence, then the sequence
(81, t1)(S2, t 2) .,. is called an infinite R1 chain over R1 UR2 . Furthermore,
if every (proper) subterm u of 8ja and tja is terminating w.r.t. R1 U R 2,
then the chain is called minimal.
In what follows, we will extend Gramlich's [Gra94] transformationwhich is defined on the set T(F) of ground terms-to hierarchical combinations. To this end, we assume that every signature has at least one

298

8. Modularity

constant c that is not used in the rewrite rules. Then for every minimal
chain that uses a substitution a : V --+ T(F, V), there is also a minimal
chain that uses a ground substitution a' : V --+ T(F). The substitution a'
is obtained from a by replacing every variable in xa with the constant c.
To assume that every signature has at least one constant c that is not used
in the rewrite rules entails no loss of generality. In fact, as in [Oh194c] one
can use a distinguished variable z instead and define the transformation on
the set T(F,{z}).

CE - Termination
The results about CE-termination until Corollary 8.6.16 are based on the
ideas of Urbain [UrbOl]. They are restricted to finitely branching TRSs.
This restriction is justified by the fact that CE-termination is not modular
for nonfinitely branching constructor-sharing systems; see Examples 8.4.188.4.20.
To start, there is the following characterization of CE-termination.
Lemma 8.6.7 A TRS R is CE -terminating if and only if there is no infi-

nite R chain over R I±J CE. In fact, if R is not CE -terminating, then there
is a minimal infinite R chain over R I±J CE.
Proof A TRS R is CE-terminating if and only if R I±J CE is terminating.
Hence the lemma follows directly from the fact that the dependency pairs
of Rand R I±J CE coincide (rewrite rules from CE do not cause dependency
pairs) in conjunction with Theorem 5.4.9.
D
In the following, we deal with a hierarchical combination (F, R) of the
base system (Fl' Rd and the extension (F2' R2). That is, R is the TRS
Rl U R2 over the signature F = Fl U F2 and Fl n V 2 = 0.
Let s E T(F) with root(s) E Fl. We use the notation s = C[Sl, ... , sn]
if C[, ... ,] E T(Fd and root(sl), ... , root(sn) E F2 \Fl . In this situation, a
reduction step s --+n t is called inner (denoted by s --+k t), if the reduction
takes place in one of the subterms Sl, ... , Sn. Otherwise, we speak of an
outer reduction step and write s --+'R t.
Furthermore, IsN(F) stands for the set {t E T(F) I every subterm t' of
t with root( t') E F2 \ Fl is terminating}.

For example, Proposition 8.6.13 can be used to show CE-termination of
the second system in Example 8.6.5. It has already been observed that the
TRS R2 of Example 8.6.3 is DP simply terminating, hence CE-terminating
by Lemma 8.3.4. The system R+ is simply terminating and thus also CEterminating by Proposition 8.3.2. Furthermore, R2 and R+ are constructorsharing, hence composable. Thus, an application of Proposition 8.6.13 yields
CE-termination of their combination.
In fact, this modularity result can be generalized to restricted proper
extensions (with a common subsystem). This generalization turns out to
be a consequence of the following theorem, which allows incremental termination proofs.

Theorem 8.6.14 Let R be the hierarchical combination of two finitely
branching TRSs RI and R 2.

1. R is terminating if base system RI is CE-terminating and there is no
minimal infinite R2 chain over R.
2. R is terminating if RI is CE -terminating and there

a quasz-

reduction ordering ;:; such that
• l ;:; r for all rules l -+ r in Rand
• s >-- t for all dependency pairs (s, t) ofR 2.

Proof (1) For a proof by contradiction, suppose that R = RI U R2 is
not terminating. According to Theorem 5.4.9, there is a minimal infinite R
chain (over R). By assumption, there is no minimal infinite R2 chain over
R. Therefore the minimal infinite R chain must contain a dependency pair
from R I , say (Sj, tj). Because the rewrite rules from RI do not contain
symbols from D2 , every dependency pair (Si' til in the minimal infinite
R chain with i ~ j must also originate from a rewrite rule of R I . In
other words, there is a minimal infinite RI chain over R. According to
Lemma 8.6.12, there is also an infinite RI chain over RIl±JCE. We conclude
from Lemma 8.6.7 that RI is not CE-terminating. This contradiction proves
statement (1).
(2) This follows directly from (1).

By means of Theorem 8.6.21, one can, for example, infer CE-termination

It follows, as in Theorem 8.4.15, that termination is modular for the
following classes of hierarchical combinations.
Corollary 8.6.22

1. Termination is modular for finite nonduplicating restricted proper extensions (with a common subsystem).
2. Termination is a modular property of finite restricted proper extensions (with a common subsystem) that are inconsistent w.r.t. reduction.
Proof Clearly, the union of two nonduplicating TRSs is again nonduplicating. Furthermore, the combination of a TRS that is inconsistent w.r.t.
reduction with an arbitrary TRS yields a TRS that is inconsistent w.r.t. reduction. Hence the assertions follow from Theorem 8.6.18 (Theorem 8.6.21,
respectively) in conjunction with Proposition 8.3.1.
0
In contrast to disjoint unions (Theorem 8.4.15), the modularity of simple termination for restricted proper extensions (with a common subsystem) does not directly follow from Theorem 8.6.18 (Theorem 8.6.21, respectively). This is because the fact that R2 is a proper extension of R1
does not imply that R2 U Emb(F2) is a proper extension of R1 U Emb(Fd.
Nevertheless, it will be shown that simple termination is indeed modular
for restricted proper extensions (with a common subsystem).

Simple Termination
Next we will review known results about the modularity of simple termination. We start with a lemma due to Gramlich [Gra96b].
Lemma 8.6.24 Simple termination is a modular property of finitely
branching composable TRSs.

Proof Let (.Ji, Rd and (F2' R 2) be simply terminating compos able TRSs.
We have to show that (F, R) = (FI U F 2 , RI U R 2 ) is simply terminating
as well. Simple termination implies that RI U Emb(Fd and R2 U Emb(F2)
are C£-terminating; see Proposition 8.3.2. Moreover, it is fairly easy to see
that these systems are also composable. Thus, an application of Proposition
8.6.13 yields C£-termination of R U Emb(F). In other words, R is simply
terminating.
0

Moreover, no example is known that shows the necessity of the condition
that no left-hand side of a rewrite rule from R2 may contain function symbols from VI U V~ strictly below its root. It should be pointed out that
Example 8.6.19 shows the necessity of this condition for C[-termination
but not for simple termination (because there R2 is not simply terminating). Therefore, it is still an open question whether Theorem 8.6.25 is also
valid for arbitrary proper extensions.

For finite TRSs, Theorem 8.6.29 can, of course, be reformulated such that

Moreover, as the system is nonoverlapping, this also proves its termination;

The following corollary to Theorem 5.6.17 shows how a finite TRS can
be split into subsystems such that innermost termination of the subsystems implies innermost termination of the whole system. In what follows,
O(P) denotes the origin of the dependency pairs in P. That is, all dependency pairs of P must originate from the set of rewrite rules O(P). (If a
dependency pair of P stems from several rules, then it suffices that O(P)
contains just one of them.) For example, for the TRS of the example in
Section 5.6.2 we have O({(5.18)}) = {f(x,c(x),c(y)) -+ f(y,y,f(y,x,y))}
and O( {(5.19)}) = {f(s(x), y, z) -+ f(x, s(c(y)), c(z))}.
Corollary 8.6.30 Let R be a finite TRS, let P 1 , ... ,Pn be the cycles in
its (estimated) innermost dependency graph, and let R j be subsystems of
R such that Un (Pj ) U O(Pj ) <;;; R j for every j E {I, ... ,n}. IfR 1 , ... , Rn
are innermost terminating, then R is also innermost terminating.

Proof Every dependency pair from Pj is an R j dependency pair because
Pj is a cycle. To see this, let (F(S), G(E)) be an R dependency pair in Pj.
Clearly, g is a defined symbol of R j because there is also a dependency pair
(G (it), H (71)) in Pj . Hence, because g is a defined symbol of R j , (F (S), G (t))
is also an R j dependency pair. Thus, every innermost R chain over R of
dependency pairs from P j is also an innermost R j chain over R. In fact,
it is an innermost R j chain over R j because Un(P j ) U O(Pj ) <;;; R j . Thus,
the corollary is a direct consequence of Theorem 5.6.17.
D
For example, in the example in Section 5.6.2 we only have two cycles,
viz. {(5.18)} and {(5.19)}. As these dependency pairs have no defined symbols on their right-hand sides, their sets of usable rules are empty. Hence,
to prove innermost termination of the whole system it suffices to prove
innermost termination of the two one-rule subsystems f(x,c(x),c(y)) -+
f(y, y, f(y, x, y)) and f(s(x), y, z) -+ f(x, s(c(y)), c(z)); cf. Corollary 8.6.30.
In fact, both subsystems are even terminating, as can easily be proven. For
the first system one can use a polynomial interpretation mapping f(x, y, z)
to x + y + z and c( x) to 5x + 1. For the second system one can use the
lpo with the precedence f ?-p sand f ?-p c. In this manner, one may use
well-known reduction orderings for innermost termination proofs of nonterminating systems.
Note that the reverse direction of the corollary does not hold. Consider
the TRS from Example 5.6.19. The only cycle of its innermost dependency graph is {(F(a(x), y), G(x, y)), (G(x, y), H(x, y)), (H(O, y), F(y, y))}.
Because this cycle does not have any usable rules, Corollary 8.6.30 states
that innermost termination of the subsystem consisting of the first three
rules is sufficient for innermost termination of the whole TRS. However, the
converse does not hold: The whole system is innermost terminating but the

312

8. Modularity

subsystem consisting of the first three rules is not; the term f(a(O), a(O))
starts an infinite innermost reduction. Conditions under which the subsystems of an innermost terminating TRS are also innermost terminating can
befound in [KROO].
Krishna Rao [KR95a] also proved that innermost termination is modular for proper extensions provided that the strict part of the dependency
relation t:::d is well-founded. Example 8.6.20 shows that the latter condition
cannot be dropped. Next, we will show that for finite TRSs Krishna Rao's
result is a direct consequence of Corollary 8.6.30.
Theorem 8.6.31 Let RI and R2 be finite innermost terminating TRSs. If
R2 is a proper extension ofR I , then their hierarchical combination RI UR 2
is also innermost terminating.

Proof As in the proof of Corollary 8.6.30, because RI and R2 form a
hierarchical combination, every cycle in the innermost dependency graph of
R consists solely of RI dependency pairs or R2 dependency pairs. If a cycle
P consists solely of dependency pairs of R I , then we have Un (P) U O(P) <:;;;
RI because dependency pairs of RI do not contain defined symbols of R 2 .
Otherwise, the cycle P consists of R2 dependency pairs. If (F(B), G(t)) is
an R2 dependency pair in P, then there is a rewrite rule f(B) -+ C[g(t)]
in R2 and f, 9 E V 2. In addition, we have f t:::d 9 and 9 t:::d f (because P
is a cycle). If 9 E V§, then f also belongs to V§, hence no defined symbol
of VI U V~ occurs in f. Otherwise, if 9 E V~, then by the definition of
proper extensions, again all defined symbols in are from V§. Thus, in
both cases, all defined symbols ofUn(G(t)) belong to V~. Hence, Un(G(t))
is a subsystem of R 2. So for any cycle P of R2 dependency pairs, we have
Un(P) U O(P) <:;;; R 2 . Hence, by Corollary 8.6.30, innermost termination of
RI and R2 implies innermost termination of R = RI U R 2.

8.6.3

Confluence

In this section, we will investigate the modular behavior of local confluence, confluence, and strong confluence. Example 8.2.1 shows that we must
impose additional restrictions, like left-linearity, to obtain modularity results for confluence that go beyond disjoint unions. To begin, we define two
notions that are crucial in this context.
Definition 8.6.33 Let Rl and R2 be TRSs.
1. Rl and R2 are orthogonal to each other, denoted by Rl .1. R 2, if
there is no overlap between a rewrite rule from Rl and one of R 2 .

2. Rl and R2 are noninterjering, ifCP(R 1 UR 2 ) = CP(Rr)UCP(R2).
Note that neither definition excludes the existence of critical pairs. If
Rl .1. R 2 , then there may be critical pairs due to overlaps between rules of
RI or rules of R 2 , respectively. If Rl and R2 are noninterfering, then there
may even be overlaps between rules from Rl and rules from R 2 ; however,
these overlaps can only create critical pairs already contained in the set
CP(Rr) U CP(R 2) of all critical pairs between rules from Rl and between
rules from R 2 . So if Rl .1. R 2 ) then the systems are noninterfering but
not vice versa. This subtle difference will turn out to be important. The
phrase "orthogonal to each other" stems from Klop [Kl092], and the phrase
"noninterfering" originates from Middeldorp [Mid90]. We point out that
the notion of "noninterference" as defined in Middeldorp [Mid90] coincides
with our notion of "orthogonal to each other" (so we have redefined the
former).
First we will show that confluence is modular for left-linear TRSs that
are orthogonal to each other.

We would like to stress that none of the modularity results presented in
this section can be generalized to proper extensions. In order to see this,

Conditional Systems

In Table 8.4 we have listed a selection of known modularity results for join
2-CTRSs (systems that may have extra variables in their conditions). In
general, the properties SIN, WIN, and WN are not modular for 2-CTRSs
(in fact, they are not preserved under signature extensions), but they are
modular for l-CTRSs; see [Gra96a]. Moreover, there are some partial results for CTRSs without extra variables. That is, some of the question
marks in Table 8.4 can be eliminated if one considers only systems without
extra variables. For example, convergence is modular for compos able conditional constructor systems without extra variables; see [Mid94a]. Special
modularity results for join l-CTRSs are summarized in Table 8.5. (Recall
that simplifying CTRSs do not permit extra variables.)
Finally, because a left-linear normall-CTRS R is decreasing if and only
if its transformed TRS Un(R) is terminating (Corollary 7.2.32), it follows
that decreasingness is modular provided the transformed system belongs
to a class of TRSs for which termination is modular; see [Mar96b]. For

In the rest of this section we will prove the first modularity results for
oriented CTRSs that have extra variables on the right-hand sides of the

We have seen that simplifyingness is modular for composable l-CTRSs.
Hence it may be a bit surprising that quasi-simplifyingness is not modular
for disjoint deterministic 3-CTRSs. The next example, taken from [Mid93,
example 4.8], turns out to be a counterexample.
